#6731. Inside the NYC Members' Club Where Republicans and Democrats Party Together

A half-dozen of us are squeezed into a small service elevator, our various colognes clashing with the signature fish-and-rubber scent of Lower Manhattan. The elevator operator blares Chinese ballads, and the young man next to me bobs his head in appreciation. An older man shifts uncomfortably. “Could you actually make it louder?” he snarks. Over the course of one excruciatingly slow ride to the sixth floor, we’re trapped in our private discomfort or pleasure. But we’re all headed to the same place: a midterms watch party at The Bench, a members’ club for people who are into politics but feel like political outsiders. It’s a place, according to its website, where “restless organizers, creatives, misfits, sneakerheads, and policy nerds collide.”

Since its founding two years ago, The Bench has hosted insiders ranging from CBS News editor-in-chief Bari Weiss to New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani. Monthly dues, “just to keep the lights on,” run as little as $50. The space is regularly used for events unaffiliated with The Bench: readings by literary magazines, climate week pop-ups, splashy debates. Michka Bengio, the social club’s owner and co-founder, is an earnest believer in dialogue and, if his programming is any indication, a little bit of a chaos agent. He likes to produce scenarios that underscore political tensions, like when he had Weiss’s The Free Press, co-host a watch party for Mamdani’s 2025 election. According to those who attended, it was a somber evening for the pro-Cuomo crowd, though some of them nevertheless returned to The Bench for this week’s midterms viewing.

On the night of the midterm elections, partygoers had the option of viewing the election results in a spacious, orderly room displaying CNN’s broadcast or in a lounge-y, narrower space screening Fox News’s coverage. In a cozy hallway between the two screens, guests could buy cocktails and popcorn or engage with people whose political views differ from their own—arguably The Bench’s most exclusive offering.

“There’s just a whole splatter of different opinions that come in here,” says David Azoulay, who works with the content agency Fixer Media. “The Free Press, the Manhattan Institute, and then I also get to meet a lot of people who are working in the Democratic space, on the Biden or Kamala Harris campaigns.” Azoulay helped produce events for an “America 250” tour in collaboration with Young America’s Foundation, a conservative youth organization, and has worked with provocateur Ben Shapiro and former Prime Minister Boris Johnson. Though he used to be a registered Democrat, he “grew up” and now identifies as a proud Republican. While Azoulay engaged in a playful back-and-forth with a progressive 21-year-old over what he considers to be the fallacy of socialism, Democratic councilmember Christopher Marte milled about wearing an “I Voted” sticker.

Over the sound of people screaming about the James Talarico-Ken Paxton race, Marte told me he met Bengio 10 years ago through Arena, a New York-based political organization that took off in the wake of the first Trump presidency. “It was all these young people trying to figure out how to get involved in politics. A.O.C. was on the couch, Alessandra Biaggi, all these people who were just curious, post-Trump election,” he says. At the time, Bengio was working as a campaign manager for Michael Basch, a techno-optimistic Democratic NYC mayoral candidate, a gig he secured by asking the only person he knew in politics to introduce them. He’s also worked as a consultant for NBA Africa and managed downtown restaurants like Regina’s Grocery and Miss Lily’s. The Bench is one of four Bengio ventures; he also co-founded the supper club Shtick with his wife, Jacqueline Lobel, and partners with the Hancock Foundation and the Hancock Cafe. On any given event night, he can be found buzzing around the room, excitedly facilitating introductions for his guests.

The son of a Moroccan father and Algerian mother, Bengio grew up in Strasbourg, France. When I ask what motivated him to move to New York in 2001, he deadpans: “Ever heard of the American dream?” More specifically, though, he was jealous of a friend who moved to the States for six months and returned speaking fluent English. “He was the coolest kid,” Bengio says. “And I was like, ‘I want to go to America, too.’ I love basketball,” he says, reorganizing a pile of books so that the edges aligned just so.

Bengio, a first-time voter, is coy about his own politics. Instead, he’s driven by the promise of America “that you can coexist,” he says, and a desire to nurture civil, democratic debate. “I want to be part of the project.”

“We need to really recommit and reaffirm what we mean when we talk about free speech—expression that is unencumbered by political interference in any way, shape, or form,” says Josef Palermo, a former curator at the Kennedy Center who drove up from the nation’s capital to attend this party. Earlier this year, Palermo wrote about the politicization of the Kennedy Center by both the Biden and Trump administrations in an article for The Atlantic. “We need spaces like this in D.C. Everybody’s in their own camps and certain venues are definitely coded one way or the other. I think having something like this would be huge in our nation’s capital.”

Then again, it’s partly New York’s distance from the D.C. swamp that makes talking about politics at The Bench feel cool. The space is decorated with Bengio’s enviable collection of NBA and political memorabilia. (He jokes that he stays up late scrolling vintage sites and bidding on Americana rarities.) Alongside Michael Jordan Wheaties boxes and a slew of rare basketball sneakers, there’s a framed copy of Emile Zola’s 1897 letter to the French youth during the Dreyfus affair, a portable Florida voting booth from the Bush vs. Gore election, a framed paper shift dress from Richard Nixon’s 1968 presidential campaign, and nearly every issue of John F. Kennedy Jr’s George magazine. The one with a Donald Trump cover gets taken down from the wall often, Bengio says, but he always puts it back up.

On Tuesday night, there appeared to be other collectors of rare political merch. Vintage Obama-era crop tops caught my eye, and a speechwriter for a New York City council member waxed poetic about his favorite vinyl record: a collection of John F. Kennedy speeches. Two guys wore t-shirts they designed themselves that cheekily quoted Michigan turncoat Haley Stevens: “Israel cums to me in my dreams.” (One of them requested anonymity, fearful of being cancelled by the Canary Mission, an anonymous organization that tracks critics of Israel.)

A good number of attendees who were professionally invested in the election outcomes couldn’t stop checking their phones. Anthony DiMieri, the man behind Mamdani’s winning social media strategy, told me he’d just come from Brad Lander’s party. Max Altman, the brother of the OpenAI founder Sam, also stopped by. He’s excited about a recent move to New York from Austin, Texas, and if he hadn’t forgotten to request an absentee ballot, he says, he would’ve voted for James Talarico.

As the results turned progressively blue, the room began to clear out. Before leaving, I chatted with Azoulay’s “libertarian friend” Hunt Beaty. In the hallway between broadcasts, Beaty tells me he’s not terribly moved by the midterms. “Everything interesting that is happening in the world right now, to me, is happening outside of politics,” he says. He rejects the libertarian designation and lives by the credo “free minds and free markets,” citing Reason magazine. And yet, Beaty finds that The Bench still has something to offer him.

“It’s fun to be in a room filled with buzzy, interesting people who at least are able to try and talk about ideas, while also being like, ‘Maybe we’re doing coke in the bathroom, too.’ I want there to be a New York City where those things could coexist.”

#6729. All the Wardrobe Essentials Every Guy Should Own in 2026

When it comes to getting dressed, we often rhapsodize about the virtues of “wardrobe essentials for men” as if everyone knows what we mean. So, let's actually talk about it.

On Maslow's hierarchy of menswear needs, wardrobe essentials might begin the perfect white tee, a killer pair of jeans, and the shoes you can wear with them. But in compiling this list, our goal wasn’t simply to meet your needs—you are, frankly, a grown-ass man, and hopefully have those covered—but more to remind you of the fundamental pieces of menswear that have helped men look hotter and feel better for generations. And in a world of non-stop trends, we need these reminders now more than ever.

In aggregate, our essentials constitute the foundation of a rock-solid men’s basic wardrobe. Below, a highly specific starter pack of everything you’ll want—nay, need—to wear in the year of our lord 2026.

A plain white T-shirt can be worn as an undershirt, standalone with some vintage jeans, or—if you want to get DIY—screen-printed into a work of art. Looking for an indie version no one else has? Check out our guide to the best white tees.

Slim, abbreviated trunks or boxer briefs that keep everything in place are the foundation of every rock-solid wardrobe—whether other people can see ‘em or not. Even if you’re in the minority that’s committed to boxer shorts, it pays to spend a little more on something that’s, you know, holding your balls all day long.

We love the zany knitwear options flooding the internet these days. But if we're honest, there will always be a spot in our closets for a classic crewneck sweater. They're timeless and endlessly style-able, even if you only manage to drape one across your shoulders.

There's a reason that the Levi's 501s is at the very top of our guide to the best men's jeans: it has been, is, and always will be the jeans you come back to again (and again, and again). If you dig your denim light and a little looser, though, Gap's Loose Jeans are a close second. Can't think of what to wear? Here's your answer, seven days a week.

If there’s another theme here, it’s that menswear essentials shouldn’t be unitaskers. They should be useful in a variety of situations, and when it comes to tailoring in particular, the navy blazer is a Swiss Army Knife. It works in almost any situation, from job interviews (with a tie) to weddings (someone else's or your own). Bonus points if you opt for a double-breasted number with strong shoulders, brash peak lapels, and an unmistakable sense of what Italians—and menswear guys—call sprezzatura.

The menswear-sphere admittedly overloaded on loafers, and we’re now seeing a slight shift in the shoe zeitgeist. But that doesn’t take away from the fact that the right pair of loafers can still be the most versatile kicks in your closet. They’ve got more range than Daniel Day-Lewis and can go from casual to dressy to punk in a flash. Wear 'em with socks or without, and count yourself lucky when they get truly worn in—then keep wearing them.

Sure, everyone knows a guy who refuses to wear shorts. But he's overpowered every time by his greatest foes: Those months of the year where, honestly, wearing anything but a short becomes a compromise. Our favorite pairs merge style with practicality, and only look better the more thrashed they get. (Except for the Champion shorts—maybe keep those a little cleaner.)

They may have emerged from the sweaty, stylish world of sportswear, but hoodies have long since transcended their athletic origins. These ones have the stamina to take you from a coffee run to casual Friday at the office (just put it under one of those jean jackets we're always telling you about).

We get that simple sneakers aren’t exactly the type of kicks you lust after on StockX, but there’s no denying the rush of dopamine you get when you break out a fresh pair, or how well they go with literally everything. At less than a Benjamin there's the Converse Chuck Taylor, a mainstay of our sneakers coverage, since, uh, always.

Not ready to take the plunge on a serious diver from Rolex or Omega? You're in luck: Seiko's much-heralded 5 series gives you everything you could want in a dive watch—an automatic movement, a day-date display, water resistance, and a near-indestructible case—at a price that won't require a second mortgage. (If you've got a few more dollars to play around with, Hamilton is no slouch, either.)

Like a plain white tee or simple white crew socks, a white Oxford shirt is a true workhorse. It can take the 9-to-5 like a champ, sharpen up for a fancier occasion, or kick back wide open over your favorite vintage tee. If you only have one shirt with buttons, let it be this.

Remember chinos? The wardrobe staple of preppies and, inexplicably, grandpas everywhere? The pant that taps in when your jeans need a break? The ones that Gap viral-ized in the late-90s? These days chinos are back and bigger than before. The chinos of today are surprisingly familiar and look more like the military-issued pairs of the midcentury—high rise and hella roomy. Some even have a pleat or two. We're all for going wide and pleated, but these both present a happy-ish medium.

While people usually start talking about them in April, sunglasses are a year-round necessity—overcast January skies be damned. They’re relevant whether you’re soaking up the summer rays, trying to cut the wintry glare, or simply trying to navigate life with a little more stealth.

Headwear trends come and go—we see you, crochet knit beanies and Trucker hats—but the baseball cap is eternal. You wear one, your dad wears one, your grandpa wears one. The solution for everything from a bad hair day, to an impromptu work Zoom, to forgetting to pack your sunglasses, they're also just plain cool, whether brand new or a little worn out. Always have been, always will.

Sweatpants should be cozy, sure, but the best of the genre are so much more than mere lazy-Sunday garb. We’ve moved past the calf-gripping elastane-pumped versions that have defined the closets of so many Lulu bros and dudes who have skate decks hung up as art. Instead, sweatpants now should be closer to the originals with a baggier fit and even wide-open hems. Spring for a pair that oozes Rocky-level charm, and they'll become the bed-to-bodega bottoms you can't stop wearing outside of the house, too.

There were a few years where the collective menswear consciousness leaned into “fun socks,” and further away from God. How easily we are led astray… Thankfully, we all now agree that the best socks are really the ones you forget that you're wearing, because they're so quietly consistent and comfortable. And in all of our years of reporting on savvy men's style choices, we've never heard a bad word said against any of the four contenders below.

Sometimes clothes can make you feel like you’re living a movie, and a good trench coat is like you’re aiming for an Oscar. (Those trucker jackets with the sewn-in hoodie are more like Hallmark). It’s the most dramatic garment you can have in your closet, and while wooden top coats are great when it's freezing out, a lighter-weight trench coat works the other nine months of the year—and can be paired with a heavy sweater for the other three. You may not be starring in any blockbusters coming up soon, but, boy, will you look like you're ready to.

In cooler temperatures, a reliable flannel shirt is more than a wardrobe essential: it's a no-brainer. Most versions will be plaid, but all of them should feel substantial enough to double as a light jacket, thanks to their lightweight wool or thick cotton make.

It's hard to think of a jacket more useful, more handsome, more versatile—heck, more all three at once—than the canvas jacket. These days, the original designs still retains their utilitarian charm, but the hammer and nails that used to accompany them have long since been replaced by a cell phone and ear buds. And thanks to fashion's ongoing workwear fixation, there's never been a wider range of luxe designer versions available.

Black derbies can pull through when you need shoes for more formal occasions—and also elevate a quick-and-dirty jeans-and-tee outfit. Dr. Martens are a great entry-level choice, and you'll get a lot more mileage out of the Mono pair, which forgoes the brand's signature yellow stitching for discreet black thread.

When your dress pants are this good—roomy and comfortable, statement-making but versatile—there's no sense in saving them for special occasions. Wear them all the time, with anything, the way you would your jeans.

Belts are often an afterthought, but they really shouldn't be. So don't waist (sorry) the opportunity. Much like cowboy boots, the western belt is riding high in fashion right now—but for this story's purposes, we'd like to guide you back to more classic styles, even if the two belts up top have a certain western flair.

By now, you're probably getting the idea that the name of the game here is versatility. While you could opt for Chelsea boots—the right pair will be able to withstand the elements, and any sideways glances from your office nemesis—you'll feel so much calmer in a pair truly designed to stomping through snowy slush.

We love that iconic North Face puffer as much as the next guy (you know the one we're talking about). But don't let its objectively perfect design distract you from the avalanche of stellar, lesser-known alternatives on the market.

A good duffel bag needs to do only one thing: hold stuff. A great duffel bag, though, needs to check off a few more boxes. It should still carry your clothes, kicks, chargers, toiletries, and everything else in the most space-efficient, easy-to-find way possible. But it should also be designed to outlast your passport—or better yet, you—despite years of being stuffed in an overhead bin and thrown in the trunk of your car. We've tested hundreds of 'em over the years, and the ones below offer all that and more.

#6727. Nobody’s Drinking, but Everyone Wants to Smell Like They Are

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

A great cocktail you drink, and it’s gone. A perfume inspired by the most perfect night out? You can wear it for years.

Fun fact: More people are wearing booze than drinking it. Or rather, they’re preferring boozy perfumes to the bar. Global alcohol consumption is historically at the lowest it’s been in 94 years (according to Gallup), but boozy perfume releases are selling in record numbers. Gen Z and younger generations are far less likely to drink alcohol nowadays, preferring low- and no-alcohol beverages and social settings, and they’re also more likely to spend more money on perfumes.

One might call it sober hedonism—I think, based on cost per ounce, it’s simply a good purchase decision. A great cocktail you drink, and it’s gone. A perfume inspired by the most perfect night out? You can wear it for years, and years to come, and instantly be transported back to that time and place. Which perfumes pull off this boozy trend? I asked some of the most discerning perfumeheads and perfumers around.

“From an evaluation perspective, a successful boozy fragrance is the one that recreates the atmosphere, the experience of enjoying a spirit. But also, the one has the right contrast and balance,” explains Jorgelina Propato, Head of Evaluation Fine Fragrance North America at Givaudan. Perfume evaluators like Jorgelina are essential to analyzing perfume drafts before they reach the consumer, working closely between the client and the perfumer. “When thinking about a boozy fragrance performance on skin, It’s important to maintain tension between the dark and light elements. When the boozy accord is connected to fresh spices, woods, aromatics, or fruity nuances, the fragrance preserves sophistication and indulgence.” Propato notes that as consumers become more sophisticated and adventurous in their fragrance choices, they’re moving past traditional sweet and fruity gourmands, and instead “gravitating toward more nuanced forms of indulgence.”

Jake Levy, co-owner of the niche perfume store Stele in New York, agrees. “You’d think New Yorkers would be over the bar scent, with all our nights out, and still we want more. My thought is that we want the booze without the hangover,” he muses. “There is nostalgia in a good night out. Booze was there for some of our best memories: a parental figure who used to sip a certain drink, or a drink tied to an experience you’ll never forget.” Levy’s picks for the category are Thin Wild Mercury’s Whisky 1969 and Art Brut’s Weltschmerz.

Cathleen Cardinali, the perfumer and owner of Thin Wild Mercury, has perhaps the most perfect bangs you’ll ever see, and her work on Whisky 1969 is as cool as she is. “Whisky 1969 is the only boozy scent you will need. It’s a lumberjack at the end of the day enjoying whisky and a solid cigarette,” notes Levy. Another of her creations, Girl of the Year, is rumored to be Chappell Roan’s favorite.

Weltschmerz is, according to Levy, “a boozy fragrance for those who hate booze. See yourself on the way to dry land, and this one will give you the memory but not the appetite.” There’s peach, cinnamon, whisky, rose, vanilla, and cedar—something quiet and moody, like a cozy but empty bar.

Steve Gontarski, manager at Luckyscent, finds that the draw to boozy fragrances can be divisive. “I’ve met some people who’ve decided to kick drinking and love wearing boozy scents as a substitute … but I’ve equally met those who avoid them because they don’t want to be reminded at all of the experience of drinking. Personally, I love boozy scents throughout the year,” says Gontarski.

He continues: “I love Bonne Chauffe from Frapin, for its perfect balance of cognac and plum. Frapin has been making cognac for generations and more recently added perfume to its portfolio: a very logical and fruitful choice.” it’s a powerful, smoky wood honoring the second distillation in cognac production. Another hitter: Eau Capitale by Diptyque, a decadent rose chypre that feels like drinking wine so dark it's nearly black. In a large, crystal goblet.”

And of course, one cannot forget the boozy perfume par excellence: Angels’ Share by Kilian. “Angels’ Share makes me want to throw on some tartan and hang out by a fireplace,” notes Gontarski. Based on sales units alone, so does everyone else: It’s a runaway bestseller. This feels completely appropriate, considering Kilian of Kilian Hennessy is, in fact, heir to the Hennessy cognac empire. He launched his perfume line in 2007, and boozy liquor notes have been woven into every perfume that debuts. Angels’ Share is a reference to the small amount of alcohol that evaporates as the liquor ages—delivered straight to the angels’ mouths.

My own favorite boozy scent is a little lesser known, but I’d love to put you on to it: Peach Tobacco Oud by Indianapolis-based Maison Saint Charles, one of the most exciting American perfume houses right now. Charles Bradshaw, a self-taught perfumer with just a few years of experience, is very talented at different presentations of boozy perfumes. My favorite of his is Peach Tobacco Oud, with brandy peaches, vanilla, orange blossom, and caramel. It doesn’t sound like it should work—and yet it does, gloriously. As a certified gourmand skeptic, it’s one of the most persuasively sexy perfumes I’ve ever tried. It is decadent and moody, sweet but not—and never—simpering. I think about it with the fondness of a lover who taught me well.

I suppose that’s the distinction I look for in gourmand, boozy perfumes: I don’t want to smell edible, but I’d like to be savored with something close to worship. Liquor notes, in just the right amount, make darkness in perfume feel delicious. They pull depth into sweetness and atmosphere to fruity florals. Peach Tobacco Oud balances the darkness with tenderness, with a self-assured panache that simply gets it right. I don’t drink brandy, but I’ll wear this one, thank you.

#6726. A Guide to Cruelty-Free Skin Care

How to spot non-animal-tested formulas and the cleansers, serums, and moisturizers to start with.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Every skin care product should be cruelty-free, but in the world of beauty regulations, rules in the U.S. neither require brands to conduct animal testing to prove a product is safe nor prohibit the practice. Brands have proudly volunteered to be “cruelty-free” certified by organizations like Cruelty Free International (Leaping Bunny) and PETA, and a growing number of countries and states have banned or restricted animal testing. You can easily identify cruelty-free certified brands by their labeling (look for the bunny). And just because it’s not tested on animals doesn’t mean it’s any less effective or safe. To jump-start your not-animal-tested lineup or to introduce a new addition to your already existing range, here are some of the best cruelty-free skin care products.

This cream cleanser foams into a rich lather, gently clears away the day with the help of Japanese rice powder, and leaves behind hyaluronic acid and red algae so skin feels clean but still hydrated.

The Tri-ceramide complex in Byoma’s multitasking mask will help restore broken-down skin barriers and reinforce those already in good shape. The peel-off treatment reduces water loss, boosts collagen production, and includes calming cica to soothe redness and irritation.

If there’s a one-and-done moisturizer to add to your routine, make it this one. The lightweight Cloud Cushion cream is loaded with brightening and texture-smoothing niacinamide, complexion-evening peptides, multiple moisturizers (squalane and snow mushroom), and ceramides to keep skin from losing moisture.

For skin in need of advanced fine-line smoothing, a texture reboot, and serious free-radical-fighting, this potent antioxidant serum—which also has moisturizing hyaluronic acid and glycerin—is the answer.

A trio of proteins works in tandem with plant-derived exosomes and growth factors to smooth fine lines, improve elasticity, and boost collagen. If refining texture is the goal, this helps with that, too.

Retinol can be tricky with sensitive skin, but this firming moisturizer pairs the ingredient with ginseng ceramides that soothe any irritation, keep skin moisturized so the retinol doesn’t dry it out, and support the skin barrier. Copper peptides in the formula help skin look bouncier.

This fan-favorite sunscreen is also cruelty-free. The skin protector goes on completely clear, has a high SPF and antioxidants, and doubles as a makeup primer.

As the name suggests, Medicube’s Zero Pore Pads use a combination of alpha hydroxy acids and beta hydroxy acids to refine, unclog, and help pores look smaller (or nonexistent). The pads also have hyaluronic acid, soothing allantoin, and the anti-inflammatory panthenol—a combo that makes it a gentler choice for sensitive skin.

Part makeup, part eye treatment, DRMTLGY’s eye corrector is formulated in four tints for light coverage, has UV-blocking zinc oxide, and has moisturizing snow mushroom to keep the delicate under-eye area hydrated and plump, not puffy.

Another double-duty skin care-meets-makeup combo is this peptide-laced bronzing serum. Tiny capsules carrying the buildable tint are suspended in the serum, which is infused with smoothing bio-peptides and cacao extract to improve elasticity.

If you’re building a cruelty-free skin care regimen, don’t forget about the lips. Mango butter and argan oil are the hero ingredients in Versed’s lip mask, which delivers a smooth, moisturized, non-sticky finish.

#6724. Crying in the Club (and Everywhere Else) With Jordan Firstman

“This is what you’re keeping me from right now,” Jordan Firstman says, and pivots his laptop screen to face a high hotel window overlooking a long, tempting stretch of Brazilian beach.

Firstman is in Rio de Janeiro for work, presenting three film-festival screenings of Club Kid, his directorial debut, which he also wrote and stars in as Peter, a dissolute downtown New York club promoter whose own extended adolescence ends abruptly when he’s called upon to parent Arlo, the 10-year-old son he’s never met.

The Brazilian audiences “really got it,” Firstman reports. It seems like everybody who watches Club Kid really gets it. The film received a seven-minute ovation at the Cannes Film Festival back in May, and sold to A24 for a reported $17 million after a brief, buzz-stoking bidding war. Preview screenings in LA, where Firstman lives, have been packed to the rafters.

Shirt and pants by Brioni. Bow tie and cummerbund, stylist’s own. Vintage watch by Rolex from Foundwell. Vintage cufflinks by Cartier from Foundwell. Earring, his own.

But when it’s suggested that this all bodes well for Club Kid’s broader appeal when it finally hits theaters, Firstman says, “Only time will tell. I hope it translates to the gen-pop.”

What we do know: Club Kid is hands down the most heartwarming film ever made about a man raising a child conceived during a blotto bisexual threesome—a supremely confident comedy-drama, raw and honest about gay sex, party drugs, and parenting in a way that’s excitingly and delightfully at odds with the sweetness of its tone. It’s an extremely likeable film, and its director-star is extremely likeable in it. And, I’ll say—at the risk of being rude—this means it’s kind of a departure for Firstman, who up until now has cultivated an almost proudly polarizing comedic persona online and onscreen. “I think, when you’re annoying and gay, people can’t get past that, in our culture,” he says, “and it’s interesting to me.”

Firstman is 35, and has not been famous particularly long, but if his public life so far has taught him anything, it’s how quickly the wind can shift. He broke through doing silly front-facing video sketches during COVID—playing banana bread’s publicist, yass-ing up his new favorite client, that sort of thing—and before long found himself apologizing for unearthed edgelord tweets he’d written in his early 20s. (He said in a statement that he’d “grown a million lifetimes” since those posts.)

His run on Fox’s English Teacher ended with the show’s cancellation in 2025; around the same time, he popped off to Vulture about the sex scenes in Heated Rivalry, suggesting the hockey bromance didn’t have a clue about “how gay people fuck.” (He is perhaps the only person ever to have pissed off that show’s fan base and lived to tell the tale, which may be evidence that nothing can stop his rise.)

Now ensconced once again as a TV-series regular—on HBO’s I Love LA—and with a buzzy movie in the pipeline, he’s able to look at all this with sunrise/sunset serenity.

“There’s ebbs and flows to whether people fuck with you or not,” he says. “Everyone loved me during the pandemic, and then everyone hated me for a little bit, and now it feels like people like me again.”

Still, he remains, by his own admission, “too online,” unable to not read things written about him, and it’s hard not to internalize some of that stuff, a little bit. He was gay and annoying in 2023’s Rotting in the Sun, playing “Jordan Firstman,” a dick-swinging, clout-chasing imp who torments a depressive artist played by the film’s director, Sebastián Silva. No less an authority than Pedro Almodóvar saw the movie and told Firstman he was a “real actor,” but his was one of the kinder reactions.

“I kind of assumed audiences would understand it was a performance,” Firstman says, “and I think many didn’t.” He thinks the movie is brilliant and calls Silva a genius. But he’s also convinced that playing a maximally annoying caricature of himself “really did affect the way people saw me, and then it affected how I saw myself. The year after Rotting was actually very tough for me, because I felt very hated.”

The new film, he says, is partly a response to that experience, he says. “I think Club Kid came from a place of, ‘I know how I feel, and I know how I’m being perceived is so different than how I feel, and I want to show a more true side of myself through this work.’”

That wasn’t how it started, of course. It started with Firstman at a dinner party, doing bits with his friends’ kid. That was the spark—Firstman realizing that putting a Jordan Firstman–type character, talking the way he does, in situations with an impressionable minor who maybe shouldn’t hear the kinds of things Firstman tends to say out loud, could be a great comedic setup.

Club Kid is in conversation with a long line of films about characters thrust into a parental role they’re ill-equipped for; Firstman can rattle them off, from Chaplin’s The Kid to Auntie Mame to Kramer vs. Kramer to Big Daddy. But as he wrote, bigger ideas emerged—about the “arrested development” of a certain cohort of childless gay men, about what it actually means to be an adult. And in working out how Peter would show up to his newfound relationship with Arlo, he thought about his own parents—two Newsday reporters who raised him in the New York suburbs—and what he got from them, as well as what he wished he’d gotten.

“I feel like no one taught me what life was going to be like. We just got by in a pleasant-ish way. But I don’t think I got major insights,” he says. A beat. “It is what it is. I am grateful that I went looking for those insights in my early 20s, through drug trips.”

This is what it’s like to talk to Firstman these days; he’s reflective, but he’ll still spring a provocative overshare on you like a trap door. (Boing! Drug trips!) The first time we met, at a photo shoot a few weeks before the call from Brazil, he told me that although the standing ovation thing at Cannes is overblown (“It felt great, but it’s such a ruse. It’s like, you can keep it going if you want. You just don’t let it stop. [From the stage] You keep pointing at different people in the movie”), the experience of showing the movie to an appreciative audience at the most prestigious film festival in the world had been transformative, that it felt like “20 years of validation that I didn’t get,” and that he no longer feels the same need to “say the edgy thing” all the time.

But at the very beginning of that conversation—as I remember it, we were still in the process of shaking hands—he started telling me an off-the-record story involving, let’s say, an adult situation. Today, over Zoom, I ask if that anecdote (explicit, slightly alarming) was the old Jordan briefly reasserting himself by saying the edgy thing.

“It probably came from a place of, maybe, nervousness, if I’m being honest,” he says. “But I think, probably, there was some sort of provocation that was a bit of a shield in that story.”

And it’s more complicated than that, he notes—because the guy who says the edgy thing is a part of him, too, and who’s to say it’s not his best self? He really does feel, after Cannes, like he doesn’t have to prove himself in quite the same way, he says.

“But then I’m like, ‘Oh, am I changing into something that’s less honest, or more honest? Are the gritty parts of me honest, and I’m letting go of them to be more palatable?’” Or is it a defense mechanism, “and the real me is something softer and sweeter and more put-together?”

The goal, of course, is to amalgamate all our disparate selves. Wouldn’t that be great? Firstman has tried his best.

“I’ve done a lot,” he says. “Everything mystical, I’ve tried.”

He’s tried past-life regression, and he’s tried ayahuasca, and he’s the second person I’ve interviewed—the first being Mike Tyson—who has tried bufotenin, a hallucinogen found in the venom of the Sonoran desert toad. (He doesn’t recall much about the first time he tried it, except a feeling “like something really bad happened”; the second time was “a way more positive, beautiful experience.”)

“I think I’ve always been a very curious person,” he says. “They say with ayahuasca, it finds you when you’re supposed to do it.”

When he heard about ayahuasca—a traditional psychoactive tea brewed from two plants native to the Amazon—on comedian Duncan Trussell’s podcast, he says, “It felt like I got shot in the stomach. I was like, ‘I have to do this.’” The next day he was at a party and somebody told him they’d just done it, under the supervision of a shaman in Topanga. Firstman went on to do it nine times over the course of his 20s.

“By the end, it kicked me out,” he says. “God, literally, was like, ‘You can’t come back here. This realm is not for you, ever again.’ I still did it one more time after that, and it was like, Bitch, are you serious? It almost really fucked me up.”

So he was pushing it, he reasons, with the toad venom. “I maybe shouldn’t have even done that, but I think it’s a different thing.”

He has this secret spiritual tool kit now, of stuff he’s learned from these experiences, about his own perceptions, and he thinks it’s protected him. He wasn’t looking for a way out of anything or relief from any particular suffering or struggle. He wanted to learn about life and what there was to experience—which, as it happens, is also how he’s approached his working life.

“I do it through film, I do it through music. I do it through social interactions. I think life is about learning in any way you can and experiencing everything you can.”

Instagram videos. Sitcoms. Directing a movie. Each another trip: What happens when I do this? He figures he could probably have built a more stable career, “if I had a more diligent respect for one medium, maybe.” But he can’t help where his creative impulses take him. Was anybody clamoring for Firstman to make 2025’s Secrets, a full-length comedy album of oddly well-executed genre-parody songs with titles like “I’m Hoarding Cum” and “Pennies in My Pussy”?

The answer is no. (The actual answer is probably “not at all.”) But once he started making the record, Firstman says, “there was no stopping me. I was in the studio, like, ‘What am I doing in a music studio?’ But I couldn’t not. I was there every day. I was begging any producer I knew to help me, because I just needed to.”

Chances are you could not make a movie like Club Kid, whose realness stems in part from a clearly lived-in understanding of partying, without having done some wild shit. But it would be difficult to make this movie if you were still living that way. Peter has Arlo to drop-kick him out of suspended adolescence and into adulthood; I ask Firstman what the catalyst was for his own growth.

“I think it was a slow process,” he says, “that’s still happening, to some degree.”

Some of what happened was professional: In 2025, he put out the album and shot the second season of English Teacher, the first season of I Love LA, and Club Kid. The practical demands of that schedule forced him to lock in: “I didn’t have Saturdays off to party, and I couldn’t afford, on a Monday, to not feel like my best.”

He also went through a breakup “that was really, really, really sad and just really unexpected, and someone I never thought I would break up with.”

He doesn’t want to be too specific about, y’know, the specifics. But it was a split he initiated.

“It came to a point where I was like, ‘I know the life I want to live and I don’t think this love is aligning with that life.’ I had to choose my life over love—which is such a mind-fuck, because you’re like, ‘Love is everything. There’s nothing more important than love.’ But then, your life is also—on a day-to-day level, it’s pretty important. And so yeah, I think it was a really tough, bizarre out-of-body experience almost, because it was a love I had never known the likes of before, and then it was over.”

By the time they broke up they had been together for four years. When it started, Firstman says, he couldn’t believe they’d found each other and the depth of what he was feeling for this person. “And I felt the exact same way with the breakup,” he says. “I was like, ‘I cannot believe this is happening.’ It feels so unreal. It just doesn’t make any sense. As much as the love didn’t make sense in the beginning, how powerful it felt, the breakup didn’t. It was the exact same feeling.

After their last conversation they agreed to stay friends, and then they didn’t talk for a year. “I think he was protecting himself and maybe protecting me too,” Firstman says. When his ex finally responded to a message, Firstman was on set, about to shoot a climactic scene in Club Kid—he’d scripted, as it happens, a big emotional moment for Peter, a moment where two characters connect on the phone after a long time apart.

“The first time, in a year, he had responded, was the moment before they called ‘Action,’” Firstman says. “That’s why I cried so hard in that scene, just like a huge release.”

His ex hasn’t seen the movie yet, Firstman says, and maybe he never will. It’s dedicated to him, though—his name is in the end credits. “He doesn’t know that either,” he says. “I’m a little scared. I’m a little scared, because to me, the movie is a love letter to him.”

He starts crying here, a little bit, apologizing even as it’s happening.

“All of that extra love that I still have for him, I put it directly into the movie. If he does see it, which I don’t expect him to or need him to, I hope he sees that’s where all my love went, and that I still have it for him and I always will.”

He has not started writing his next movie yet, but he’s started thinking about it. He knows that like this one, it’ll be a love story—and that after writing, in Club Kid, about familial love, community love, and loving yourself, he wants to write about friendship next.

In the past year, he’s been blown away by two movies of vast scope, Paul Thomas Anderson’s One Battle After Another (“I was making my movie when that came out, and I think that is a movie, for him, about growing up, and not feeling like a grown-up as an adult”) and the equally sprawling queer epic La Bola Negra. I ask him if he’s looking to go similarly big with his next project. Maybe, he says.

“But I think I’d like to take incremental steps and learn more as a filmmaker before.…”

“Yeah,” he says, laughing. “My war movie. The Firstman war movie. I have zero context for war, so I would be completely making everything up.” ✦

#6723. Reformation’s Next Chapter Is Happening at Home

Because good style doesn’t end in your closet.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Reformation fans have cause to rejoice today, as the beloved brand just announced its first-ever foray into home goods. This morning, Reformation launched its collaboration with French rugmaker Les Editions de Tapis.

Lauren Caris Cohan, chief creative officer at Reformation, has worked with Les Editions de Tapis for years, bringing to life rugs for Reformation stores. According to Cohan, customers have a habit of asking where the rugs are from. Now, they can bring them home for themselves.

The inspiration for the designs is rooted in French elegance and L.A. nonchalance, according to Cohan. “I think that combination is very Ref: something romantic and considered, with enough ease that it never feels precious,” she tells ELLE. “There’s a sense of humour, but it’s subtle. A rug can be a serious piece of craftsmanship and still have a cat walking across it. That balance feels very true to Reformation.”

The collection comprises nine made-to-order rugs, three of which are chintz, designed in collaboration between Cohan and Les Editions de Tapis founder Thomas Coccimiglio.

“Thomas and I have a longstanding creative partnership,” Cohan told ELLE. “Les Editions de Tapis has made exquisite rugs for our stores for nearly five years now, and we get such lovely feedback about those spaces. Bringing a special piece of that experience into our customers’ homes felt like a natural next step.”

Each rug is hand-knotted by artisans in India and Nepal. Wool, linen, and silk are featured throughout. The rugs are exclusively available online, and prices range from $1,000 to $5,000. Shop them all below and at thereformation.com.

#6720. Kevin Costner Is Particular About Scents—So He Made His Own Colognes

Eight years ago, Kevin Costner was in Germany on a press tour. His hotel room was chock full of fruit baskets and other fancy accouterments that come with international fame. And also a handful of bath products. “I’m taking a shower and I’m thinking, ‘Man, this body wash smells good,’” he tells me from the couch of his modern white compound on a seaside street in Carpinteria, California. “And I’m thinking, ‘I don’t really use those words. Why would I even think that to myself?” Maybe two more times on that 15-day junket, he found himself thinking about the scent of that body wash.

“Can’t tell you why, don’t even know what it was,” he says. “I came back home to America, and probably over the next year, periodically, I actually thought about it. But then it faded in like a song you can’t remember.”

A few years later, he found himself in Scotland, scoring his Western epic Horizon. After a jetlagged detour to a countryside castle, he hopped in a hotel shower in Glasgow, and there it was: the smell. “I looked down and there were the bottles and I go, ‘That’s that smell from five or six years ago. That’s that smell,’" he recounts with his English lab, Bob, curled up at his side. “And I did something incredibly stupid. I went out of the shower and I got my phone.”

He got water everywhere in the process, but at that moment he was singularly focused. “I go back in the shower and I take a picture of these bottles as if they were going to disappear. I could have just really finished my shower, honestly. Dried off like a normal human being. But what I’m saying was it fired my senses again, imagination. And I sent those pictures back and I said, ‘See if you can get a case of this. I’m never going to forget it again.’”

It turned out to be 19-69’s Rainbow Bar, a scent inspired by both the ‘80s glam rock bands that frequented the Rainbow Bar on the Sunset Strip, along with general notes of California: there’s bourbon alongside basil and vetiver, creating a fragrance that’s simultaneously woody and green. That kickstarted Costner’s career as a perfumer. Now, he’s debuting a collection of fragrances with the same Milan-based niche perfume house. The collection consists of three fragrances (or extrait de parfum, to be precise), chicly advertised on 19-69’s website with a photo of Kevin Costner’s star on the Hollywood Walk of Fame. They retail at $80 per 7.5 milliliter bottle (a 30 ML bottle will run you $300, and the really giant 95 ML version costs a cool $800), and each fragrance—called After Hours, Heritage, and Pacific Coast Highway—reflects something of Costner’s life and history.

Kevin Costner first discovered 19-69 after using its body wash during a press tour in Germany, but it wasn't until years later he figured out the brand's name.

He tells me this story from his living room, amid coffee table books on design and the American frontier, and a homemade scrapbook dedicated to Bob’s first year of life (the dog peed a little bit on my foot, but I took it as a compliment). I visited on a foggy day in June, walking into his home to be greeted by a view of the Pacific. Costner, who has an endless string of massive, monocultural hits—Field of Dreams, The Bodyguard, the tv show Yellowstone—has the aura of a Paul Newman-type, his bright blue eyes undiminished by the trail of drool and dog hair that Bob left on his feather-light black sweater. The peculiarity that we are talking about his collaboration with 19-69, and not an upcoming film of his, is not lost on him. “I bring just a lot of feeling to things because I can’t always explain why I like something,” he says. “It’s like wine. I think, ‘oh, it smells like wood.’ But does it, really? The fuck it does.”

After his second encounter with 19-69 products in Scotland, Costner’s assistant eventually tracked the brand’s co-founder Johan Bergelin down, and 19-69 sent him a case of the product. In 2024, he called Bergelin, to congratulate him on a job well done. They met in person for the first time that August in Cologne, Germany, which was not on purpose but did make them laugh. Shortly after that first meeting, they went ahead with a partnership, spending two years developing a collection of fragrances, produced in France.

As a child growing up in a small town in the Skåne region of Sweden, Bergelin had, much like Costner, been seduced by a scent he encountered in the wild. His was a perfume at the local hair salon, a mass market scent from Karl Lagerfeld. An artist and designer, he eventually became a self-taught perfumer, and launched 19-69 in 2017 with his wife, Catharina, who handles communications and writes the colorful copy for each scent. The Milan-based brand—initially inspired by the culture and societal upheaval of 1969, the year of Bergelin’s birth—is known amongst self-described “fragheads” for its offerings tied to specific eras and places (Christopher Street, the Rainbow Bar in Hollywood, the island of Capri): their Miami Blue scent has a note of cocaine; the American Psycho perfume, a collaboration with Bret Easton Ellis (which just won an Allure “Best of Beauty” award), contains hints of palate-cleansing sorbet and harkens back to the products in Patrick Bateman’s prophetic 1980s beauty routine; Female Christ references a 1969 performance piece by Danish artist Lene Adler Petersen, in which she walked naked through the Copenhagen Stock Exchange carrying a cross. For the Costner collection, they’ve developed a group of three scents, titled After Hours, Pacific Coast Highway, and Heritage, all based on the actor’s life.

After Hours, the signature scent of the collection, has notes of cypress, bourbon, and nutmeg; Bergelin describes as a version of 19-69’s Rainbow Bar fragrance “on steroids,” and says that for him, it evokes the image of someone in a glass house in the Hollywood Hills at sunset, surveying the city. It reminds Costner of shooting one of his first major magazine covers, the May 1989 issue of Vanity Fair, with the louche, legendary Helmut Newton. “So, I was in a tuxedo but my tie was off and my jacket was on the couch,” he recounts. “It was supposed to be a sexy shot with a glass of whiskey. And Helmut kept saying, ‘take your shirt off, just leave the bow tie on or something’... I said no. I’m not an exotic dancer!” He carefully notes that Newton was “so sweet” and did not make this suggestion in any sort of predatory fashion. He was merely opposed to looking like a Chippendale.

Costner (left) first met 19-69 co-founder Johan Bergelin (right) in 2024 and together they spent two years developing a trio of fragrances inspired by the actor's life.

Costner once wore After Hours to one of his son’s volleyball tournaments. “There were about 15 teams of teenage boys,” he says. “So the gym smelled really bad.” He says a pregnant woman kept staring at him, with her friend tugging her arm, urging her not to approach. “She walks all across the gym towards me,” he says. “And I wasn’t sure what this was going to be about. And she just turns to me and says, ‘Look, I’m married, I’m not [trying to bother you]. But what are you wearing?’” She did not ask for an autograph.

“The scent doesn’t have to be obvious,” he says. “But I love that it somehow becomes noticeable on its own merit, period.”

Pacific Coast Highway is lighter, with notes of clementine, juniper berry, and jasmine, Bergelin’s favorite. It smells like going to the beach in California (on a day without an algae bloom or whatever) and then taking a long hot shower after. It is clean without being boring, and I love it. Costner says the PCH scent reminds him of Tommy James and the Shandell’s 1968 hit “Crystal Blue Persuasion,” a lysergic earworm inspired by the Books of Isaiah, Ezekiel, and Revelations that most listeners and critics instead took to be a song about acid, amphetamines, or meth. “It’s easy,” he says. “A light sensory moment.”

Heritage is a rich, masculine scent that nods to life on Costner’s sprawling ranch in Aspen, Colorado, with notes of cherry, violet leaves, amber, and vanilla, scents that sound sweet and girly on paper but add up to something whiskey-ish (“It’s on the richer side,” says Bergelin. “It has volume.”) Costner says it reminds him of “a place that’s very masculine, carpets and wood and guns and there’s a deepness to it like the wood of a place. It has a forest-y feeling to me.”

Costner, an avid hunter, is eager to talk about guns as we sniff Heritage. He both believes in laws that limit them and sees them as heirlooms, like jewelry. “If you misuse a gun, you should never have one again,” he says. “But I have my father’s gun, and he got it when he was young. And so, when I put that gun to my cheek, I can feel him. When I was a boy, I would just ask him to let me hold it and it was much too heavy for me. And now it feels almost light as a feather. And my sons will one day put the gun to their cheek when I won’t be here, and they’ll remember those times in the field with that dog and everything else.”

19-69’s tagline is “bottling counterculture.” But Costner, born in 1955, had a different experience of the hippie years. He was raised in a conservative household without much money in Southern California; his late brother left to serve in the Marines in Vietnam while other kids his age protested the war. He rigorously studied classical piano with an elderly instructor who had been around long enough to teach FDR. “You can imagine where the politics were with my mother and father, who wanted to support their son in Vietnam,” he says. “At the dinner table, long hair was bad. The music was bad. They didn’t like anything about it. And I was sitting there eating at that same table thinking I liked a lot of it.”

He was too young to go full Woodstock. But he remembers what it was like to be around the culture. “A whole generation here went north to the Haight-Ashbury, to San Francisco, the Summer of Love,” he remembers. “I would watch the last exit out of Santa Barbara when there was no freeway. It was just a highway with lights, and the last one was like a last man out of Saigon moment. There would sometimes be as many as 60, 70 kids at that light with their thumbs out.”

Costner’s best-known roles are visceral and sexy, and in watching them, it’s easy to conjure up scents: saddle leather, the grass of the Great Plains and baseball parks, sweat, campfires, gunpowder. Millions of people have formed their own relationships with him by going to the movies, surrounded by the scents of melted butter and spilled soda. One 2016 study examined the olfactory experience of going to the cinema, in which chemists asserted that audiences emitted different compounds, like carbon dioxide and isoprene, depending on the genre of film they were watching, unwittingly creating new scents for emotional or romantic scenes. If that’s true, which seems awfully nice to believe, Costner has, in one small way, been in the fragrance business for a long time.

He says his favorite smells are lemon, cinnamon, and sage (“I knew [sage] as a hunter, not as an in-crowd thing”), and his house smells like cleanliness, or nothing. I asked if he likes the smell of movie theaters. “Not particularly,” he says. “I think what I miss in a theater is the curtain. But I know that smell of popcorn. It’s almost ceremonial, in a way, and it’s always lousy. It’s better when you cook it at home, it just is.”

#6719. 22 Killer Watches I Saw at One of New York’s Most Exclusive Collector Hangs

This is an edition of the newsletter Box + Papers, Cam Wolf’s weekly deep dive into the world of watches. Sign up here.

I knew the watch collector meetup I was going to was legit when its organizer sent this text to the group: “We’ve engaged an off-duty NYPD detective to work security for our dinner. So, now really no reason not to bring your best!”

Last Wednesday night, at a fancy steakhouse in Midtown Manhattan, I was reminded why I love these enthusiast-organized events. This one was relatively small in comparison to blowouts like Rolliefest, but the idea was the same: A dozen or so collectors brought along their most prized timepieces and plunked them down on the table unattended (besides the off-duty NYPD detective, of course). When I asked one attendee if I could take his Breguet—similar to one heading to auction this weekend with a $73,000 estimate—across the room to take a photograph, he reacted like I’d asked if I could breathe oxygen: Of course! What a silly question. Events like these genuinely reflect the best of the hobby: deeply passionate collectors who really just want to share their watches with like-minded obsessives.

Between bites of miniature beef wellingtons and medium-rare steak, the guests passed around and ogled the dozens of grails on display. There were new classics on the table—including Vacheron Constantin’s Ultra-Thin, a leading contender for watch of the year—alongside plenty of old ones, including every flavor of vintage Rolex, from GMT-Masters to Daytonas to Explorer IIs. I was standing with one collector as he examined a pretty vintage Rolex GMT-Master. He stopped and looked around. So-and-so must be here, he muttered aloud. How did he know? Because he recognized the shade of the patina on the dial.

This particular group started getting together a couple of years ago, after the collectors @HorologicallyMinded, @s_h_lemon, @null_islands, and @jmsfisk (everyone at the event asked to remain anonymous) met at another watch-related retreat. @HorologicallyMinded, or HM, says that collecting was at one point something he did in solitude. “People generally judge when they learn how much watches cost,” HM tells me. “You paid how much for that!?” But he and the other three founders began meeting up for monthly dinners before eventually taking turns to organize these larger events. “This group has been the most rewarding part of this hobby,” HM says. “It’s been so great to have a group of people that have a common interest, but it’s not work-related and no one judges.”

Although the group has grown, the core four are still instrumental in making sure the functions go off without a hitch, with just the right spread of watches on the tables. “It is very deliberate and the four of us generally coordinate on it,” HM says. “We specifically make sure there is something for everyone.” That means ensuring there are at least a handful of watches worth less than $10,000—this time, that included several Rolex Datejusts, a Vulcain Cricket, and a gold Breitling from the 1940s. “We knew we were having some relative newbies and different people have different budgets, and we want everyone to be comfortable.” On the other end of the spectrum, some grail-level pieces were special requests from other members of the group.

All the watches wound up getting plenty of TLC. “I love the fact that we’re a group that can, and did, totally geek out about a vintage Longines Legend Diver, while a Doré dial Patek is on the same table,” HM says.

Below are some of the very best pieces I saw that night.

A Patek Philippe ref. 3970, part of the house’s historic lineage of pieces that combine a perpetual calendar with a chronograph. The dial on this one is so tasty.

A skeletonized Breguet Ref. 3355PT00PA0—maybe the watch of the night for me—on a show-stopping platinum bracelet.

A few years ago, MB&F started making more approachable versions of its sci-fi-ready watches and allocating them through a lottery system. This is the M.A.D2, with an outer ring on the dial that spins around very quickly. It’s really fun.

The aforementioned Rolex GMT-Master is a ref. 16760, a model produced during the ’80s that’s nicknamed the “Sophia Loren” thanks to its curvaceous profile.

Omega has incredible stuff in its archives. This is a chronograph from the ’30s that’s controlled entirely through that single button at 2 o’clock.

A couple of real bangers here. The Vacheron on the left is known as the “Cioccolatone,” because it resembles a square of chocolate. At the center is the Patek Philippe ref. 3417, an antimagnetic watch with just about the prettiest typography you’ll ever see on a watch. Zohran Mamdani should adopt that cursive for signs in the city, because it can make just about anything look nice: Warning: Rats. Or maybe, No Dumping Here.

A cloisonné dial from—of all brands—IWC! I am such a sucker for a cloisonné, which refers to the intricate images carefully fashioned from wire and colorful baked enamel.

A beautiful example of one of the Seafarers that Heuer made for Abercrombie & Fitch from the ’50s through the ’70s, back when the latter was still a rugged outdoor outfitter. The shades of orange, yellow, and blue are dreamy.

A monochromatic take on the Audemars Piguet Royal Oak Perpetual Calendar, which was produced in the ’80s and ’90s but looks incredibly modern.

Universal Genève finally relaunched last year after decades on the shelf, but it’s hard to beat the originals. This Polerouter comes on an extremely cool “wave” bracelet, which basically uses a repeating chevron pattern to make the slinky bit of metal appear to be in motion.

During World War II, a dozen watch manufacturers came together to make models following a strict set of specifications for the British Ministry of Defence. One of those brands was Longines, which produced this strapping piece.

You didn’t think we’d wrap this list up without a gorgeous yellow-gold Daytona, did you?

The ultimate fusion of sport and luxury watches. Heuer supplied this ’70s-era Carrera to Ferrari’s racing team, including legendary drivers like Jacky Ickx, Niki Lauda, and Clay Regazzoni.

This Patek is cool, but the bracelet is insane.

This isn’t your average Patek Philippe Nautilus. PP began producing this reference 3800 in 1981 as a smaller counterpart to the original. The few times I’ve gotten to try one on, I’m always struck by how perfect the proportions are. It’s nice and thin and looks just right on the wrist.

The United States Merchant Marines’ logo really punches up this Day-Date.

The ref. 6541 is the most beloved version of the Rolex Milgauss. It was produced in the ’50s and features a signature honeycomb dial. One sold for $2.5 million at auction in 2023.

Is there a better way to time your yacht race than with this Heuer Skipper? Probably not.

It’s good to have doubles, especially when it comes to vintage Rolex Explorer IIs.

#6718. The Tommy Hilfiger Gifts Worth Giving

A little alpine spirit goes a long way, especially when it comes wrapped and ready to give.

Finding a great holiday gift means knowing what someone will actually love receiving—and ideally, using long after the decorations come down. For the friend who looks forward to a weekend in the mountains, that might mean a cozy knit or a warm accessory. For the frequent traveler, a polished wallet or a toiletry bag could be just the thing. And sometimes, a little fragrance is all it takes to make a gift feel special.

Tommy Hilfiger has been putting its own spin on American classics since 1985, drawing on the easy confidence of East Coast prep and the sporty influences that have long defined its look. This heritage feels right at home in the brand’s alpine campaign, where familiar staples take on a winter-ready appeal. The result is a gift selection that moves from cozy cold-weather favorites to thoughtful little luxuries, with plenty of Tommy’s signature style.

A cable-knit sweater is a gift she’ll reach for all winter. This lightweight cotton version has a relaxed, easy fit, classic crew neck, and ribbed trim. The traditional knit gives it a timeless appeal, while the soft pink shade adds a fresh touch. It’s an easy choice for everything from a weekend in the mountains to a casual day around town.

For the friend who always feels a chill, this knit hood is a fun alternative to the usual winter hat. The design features a monogram logo for a signature touch and offers extra coverage for cold-weather walks, snowy weekends, and everything in between.

Gloves are easy to overlook when shopping for gifts, which makes a good pair all the more welcome. This wool-blend knit version is a gift with staying power, adding a classic finishing touch to a cold-weather look. Tuck them into a gift box for someone planning a mountain escape, or give them to the friend who never leaves home without checking the temperature first.

This trifold wallet gets its personality from a buckle accent and monogram detail, giving the compact design a polished finish. It’s a nice option for someone who likes a bit of branding without carrying a logo-heavy bag, and its smaller profile makes room for the rest of the essentials.

There’s something especially nice about unwrapping a new fragrance during the holidays. Tommy Hilfiger’s Freedom comes in a generous 3.4-ounce bottle, making it a gift with staying power.

This monogram-print toiletry bag from Tommy Hilfiger gives skin care, makeup, and other travel necessities a dedicated place, while the allover pattern makes it easy to spot in an overstuffed suitcase. Consider it a small upgrade for the friend who always has another trip in mind.

#6716. Taylor Swift and Travis Kelce Re-Created One of Their First Public Moments After the Chiefs’ Win

Taylor Swift and Travis Kelce made a rare move following the Kansas City Chiefs’ win over the New York Jets yesterday. The newlyweds left Arrowhead Stadium together, re-creating one of their first public moments as a couple by driving off in a vintage Cadillac Coupe DeVille. Onlookers filmed the happy couple waving to the crowd as they departed.

When Swift attended her first-ever Chiefs game in September 2023, the pair did the same thing, leaving in Kelce’s 1970 Chevrolet Chevelle 408 Stroker convertible. They seem to have a thing for the classic cars, and even raffled off a 1970 Chevelle at their wedding, an apparent homage to the 2023 moment.

The singer attended Kelce’s game against the Jets with her parents and her friend Karlie Kloss. It marked Swift’s first appearance in years with Kloss, who also attended the singer’s wedding in July.

Swift usually leaves Arrowhead Stadium undetected. Back in October 2025, she told BBC Radio 2’s Greg James that her greatest skill as a spy would be “entering and exiting the building without being seen. Just put me in a garbage can, roll me, I don’t care. Honestly, I can fit in like, a purse. Sometimes I just can’t deal with it [the paparazzi and attention] and in those times, I won’t deal with it. I’m just digging tunnels under every building I go into, airlifting in through the skylight.”

In January 2025, Parade spoke with Ty Richmond, president of event services at Allied Universal, who detailed how intricate plans can be to get VIPs in and out of football games. “When you have a very high-profile individual [coming to an event], the logistics of that person getting into the venue and getting into the specific location where they need to sit is a job and a process in itself,” he said, adding that for someone at Swift’s level of fame, thousands of security professionals could be involved in days of preparation.

“If, for instance, there are fans that are known to be targeting [a star] and/or have been a threat in the past because they’re fixated on a talent or a personality, those are all very specialized, very confidential issues that have to be understood and mitigated properly,” he added.

#6714. Martine Rose Is Balenciaga’s New Creative Director

Martine Rose is Balenciaga's new creative director. The London-based designer will start on November 16, taking over the French-Spanish label from Pierpaolo Piccioli, whose departure was announced yesterday after three seasons at the house.

It's the first time Martine Rose has taken charge of a major fashion house, but it isn't her first Balenciaga gig. She worked as a menswear consultant under Demna from 2015 to 2018, helping shape the oversized proportions and left-field clothes that defined his early years at the brand. Now she's returning with control of the whole enterprise.

“It is a true privilege to be appointed creative director of Balenciaga, a house with an extraordinary heritage and a fearless creative spirit,” Rose said in a statement. “Balenciaga has been shaped by remarkable creative talents before me. I am committed to honoring this legacy and look forward to creating the next chapter in this rich and beautiful story.”

Rose founded her eponymous label in 2007 and has built it into one of the most influential brands in British fashion. Her collections pull from soccer and nightlife club culture, music, and the everyday wardrobes of London dwellers: suits get stretched out or squeezed in, tracksuits become tailoring, loafers turn into mules. It's menswear, on her terms.

Through her career, Rose has collaborated with Nike, Clarks, Supreme, Stüssy, and Tommy Hilfiger, and her clothes have been worn by everyone from Kendrick Lamar to Lewis Hamilton to Timothée Chalamet. But despite her idiosyncratic influence over what menswear looks like now, Rose has yet to have worked the top job at a house of Balenciaga's size. Her appointment makes her the first woman to lead the brand in its 109-year history. Her appointment makes her the first woman to lead the brand in its 109-year history.

“Martine Rose embodies this spirit with a fresh, fearless and deeply contemporary vision,” said Kering CEO Luca de Meo. “Her connection to the new generation and her instinctive understanding of culture bring a new dimension to the house.”

It's a big appointment, and one fashion people have wanted for a long time. Rumors that Rose had signed with Balenciaga have been swirling for weeks, but now it's official. The house hasn't yet said when her first collection will arrive, but whenever it does, we'll all be watching.

A version of this story originally appeared in British GQ.

#6713. 7 Ways to Wear the Jeans-and-a-Blazer Look for Fall and the Most Versatile Styles to Shop Now

It’s a longstanding partnership for good reason.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The idea of a uniform isn’t new. In fact, that’s kind of the point: it prioritizes a sense of timelessness that defies trends. The winning partnership that emerged from recent street style sightings, however, felt newly relevant—not a flash in the pan coupling, but one that has what you might call an unshakable bond: jeans and blazers.

The beauty of this particular uniform is that it doesn’t come prepackaged. Instead, you can pick from a plethora of different jeans—are you a barrel-leg, capri, or boot-cut kind of person?—and then opt for the blazer that best suits its proportions.

Keep scrolling for the seven combinations that have been cherry-picked for the insouciant energy that’s part and parcel of the jeans-and-blazer look.

Military jackets were spotted across the street style scene during SS27 shows. Combining the statement piece with casual denim works to balance out the look that will work well into winter.

Here, a pair of ’70s-inspired, front-pocket jeans are the perfect match for a double-breasted, cropped blazer. Plus, choosing a straight-leg silhouette helps to accentuate the padded shoulders of the jacket, allowing a simple look to transform into a statement.

A smarter pair of baggy jeans—either because of a darker wash or lack of distressing at the knees—is the perfect partner for a suede jacket, which requires no introduction after its dominance on the pavements of fashion month.

For a super-polished take on the combination that’s suitable for work (and whatever comes after), a scarf blazer paired with straight-leg jeans is the ultimate power couple.

Like capri pants, Bermudas are still a viable option in autumnal temperatures. As Gabriella Karefa-Johnson shows, you’ll want to wear your pair with a nearly knee-high boot, especially when it contrasts with a patchworked top half that limits the amount of bare skin exposed. Smart.

The best way to remove the slouch factor from cuffed jeans? Add a single-breasted pinstripe blazer with one button fastened.

The curvaceous nature of a barrel-leg jean strikes a brilliant contrast with a blazer that’s cropped to either the waist or hipbone, especially if, like this streetstyler, you keep the rest of your look pared-back enough to let its sculptural lines sing.

#6712. Erdem Is on a Red Carpet Style Streak—Here’s Why

Celebrities from Kim Kardashian to Cynthia Erivo to Troye Sivan have pulled looks

At London Fashion Week this September, Erdem debuted a Spring 2027 collection that was instantly celebrated for its very on-brand tug-of-war between masculinity and femininity. Individual ensembles clashed crystal embellishment, tuxedo details, classic shirting, and unexpected flashes of skin. Critics praised it—and celebrity stylists began calling pieces in almost immediately.

Erdem has long had a slew of celebrity friends, supporters, and customers, but something about the reception this season feels different. In less than two months, runway looks have been worn by Kim Kardashian, Rosalía (twice), Cynthia Erivo, Sienna Miller, Troye Sivan, Honey Dijon, Cara Delevingne, Chappell Roan, Inde Navarette, Rosé, Keira Knightley, and Sarah Paulson. An unlikely collective perhaps, but each individual puts forth their own glorious interpretation of style and gender expression.

Erdem Moralıoğlu began this collection with the story of Barbette, the Texas-born trapeze artist whose performances, done in full drag, rose to prominence in Paris in the 1920s and ’30s. The clothes explore this sort of complex, androgynous dialogue between masculinity and femininity. The idea is perhaps best epitomized in a dress (worn by Knightley) that begins as a menswear-inspired gray knit before fading into a crystal-embellished sheer tulle skirt—and styled in the very Erdem manner with socks and black brogues. Or take Sivan’s ensemble: a black tuxedo jacket with satin and tulle draped over an ostrich feather pannier at the hip. Even the most markedly girlish looks, like Rosalía’s ’20s-inspired dress in blush pink silk, come with a certain restraint.

Moralıoğlu celebrated 20 years of Erdem this year and has built a ready-to-wear brand that is both distinctly recognizable and still able to evolve each season. Spring 2027 was not a radical departure for the designer, merely a looser approach to his typical decadence. (After all, Moralıoğlu has always paired his colorful jacquard dresses with simple black brogues.) It seems like a literal snapshot of consumers and their desires: clothes that combine elements of femininity and masculinity, utility and flourish. And it proves that two decades in, Moralıoğlu can continue to woo new audiences.

Ahead, we spoke with Moralıoğlu to gauge his reaction to all of this attention and reflect on what’s resonating most.

Well, the muse, as you know, was someone named “Barbette,” or Vander Clyde Broadway, who was originally from Austin, Texas, and then came to Paris and became this extraordinary muse and then kind of disappeared into obscurity. I love the idea of this idea of someone becoming something and that idea of self-actualization. But also, more importantly, Cocteau wrote a very interesting essay on Barbette and commissioned Man Ray to do this beautiful photo essay on him, as well. It was really about this idea of how relative masculinity and femininity is and about androgyny. So if I had to summarize it into one thing, there's a feeling of androgyny and flitting between masculine and feminine.

Yes, and I think what's interesting about Barbette as a character was that it was equally as feminine as it was masculine. In a way, they weren't trying to emulate becoming a woman, but they also weren't emulating a man. It's almost like the third sex, which is, I think, very interesting.

I definitely have noticed it, and it's definitely been noticed in the studio. All of us are like, "Wow, another one! It really seems to be happening.” But it's such a privilege to dress so many different people from so many different backgrounds and extraordinary, amazing, brilliant, talented people. I mean, it's wonderful. It's thrilling. I love it.

I mean, I can't pick just one. But I thought it was so interesting to see Troye in that piece, to see how that all worked and how it evolved. Interestingly enough, I had originally created trousers for that look, but didn’t use them for the show. So then the trousers came back out for that carpet. Otherwise, Troye was so loyal to how it was shown on the runway, even with the shirt styled exactly the same.

I still feel the same way. I think it's a privilege to be in a position where you dress extraordinarily talented people. That's a wonderful extension of your world that then becomes something else. It’s really interesting to see the collection take on another life. Otherwise, it just exists in the bubble of a show or an editorial.

The honest answer would be no, I don't think about it at all. I think about the collection and the character—her or them or whoever that person is. But when the show's coming together, you also really think about movement, how it's fitted, how something really moves on the body. And I wonder if that's something that's interesting in the context of it being outside of a runway show. I find Rosalia walking down the street in New York so fascinating. They're clothes that move and I really like that.

Well, I just left the other room where we’re working on the next one. But I like that psychology of turning the page. I find that really interesting. I love the idea of the blank, white page. I find it really exciting. But honest answer, no. [I don’t think about evolution]. Maybe what happens is that in time, you become a little bit looser. With that last show, there was something about that kind of world of Barbette. It was easy for us in the studio to really feel it, feel that masculine and feminine energy and to be very loose with it.

I usually tend to have a character. I always find it's like making a movie every six months. It's almost like a new script. I think it helps to take everyone collectively somewhere else. So you have to adjust together, but you have to have something to take everyone there with you.

Too difficult to say. In the context of these extraordinary women…Cynthia, I just think is amazing. Or Inde. And then Rosalía, Chappell, I'm like…

Let's think. Dietrich. [Marlene] Dietrich would be a good one. I love her.

Well, I think there’s something interesting about this idea of how you have a starting point and then you can end up somewhere else. How do you take the idea of something, and make it modern. How are you looking at something and finding its spirit? When you think about all of these questions of gender and expression, it's something that's always existed culturally. That's also a really beautiful thing.
